Well, it's... it's a whole lot of things, really, but my manager at the restaurant told me I had to come see someone or I couldn't come back to my shifts. I've been waitressing for years, you know? Since I left college. I know the menu back to front, I know the regulars, I know how the floor moves, but lately... lately it's like the wires are just getting crossed. And what really scared everyone at work, what made them pull me off the floor on Tuesday, is that I started just... freezing up in the middle of service. Like, totally stuck. Or I'd start doing things completely out of order. I was supposed to be closing out a party of six, but instead, I was just standing by the soda station. I was holding the key to the cash drawer, just gripping it, and I kept repeating table numbers out loud—"table 42, table 12, table 42"—instead of actually going to the register and closing their checks. And I knew I had the key, and I knew I needed to do the checks, but I just couldn't make my body do the next step, and I just kept saying the numbers over and over while the ice machine was humming, and the manager had to physically come take the key out of my hand because I was just... stuck there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It wasn't really a fear driving it, no. It was... well, like I was just saying, it was just completely out of my control. That's the part that really scared everyone, and me too, honestly. The freezing up. Because normally, closing checks is just pure muscle memory, right? You get the cash, you take the key, you go to the till, you make the change. It's a dance. But standing there by the soda station... it was like the connection between my brain and my feet just completely severed. 

I was holding that cash drawer key so tight my knuckles were white, and I could feel the little metal ridges on it, and the little plastic tag, but I couldn't make myself actually walk the fifteen feet over to the register. I was just doing it all completely out of order. Instead of actually closing the checks like I was supposed to, my mouth just started making the sounds of the tables I needed to close. 'Table 42, table 12.' Just spilling out of me. I didn't *want* to be saying them out loud like that, and I didn't have some specific scary thought making me chant them. I just... I couldn't move to the next step of the task. 

It's the middle of service, the absolute busiest time of the Tuesday dinner rush, you know, when everyone's trying to get out before the theater across the street starts, and I'm just glued to the floor next to the ice machine. Completely stuck, doing everything out of order, just repeating those numbers into the air while the tickets are piling up and the other girls are trying to squeeze past me with trays of drinks. I was just trapped in it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.2, F06.1, F42.8, F44.89, G40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a whole lot of things, really, but my manager at the restaurant told me I had to come see someone or I couldn't come back to my shifts. I've been waitressing for years, you know? Since I left college. I know the menu back to front, I know the regulars, I know how the floor moves, but lately... lately it's like the wires are just getting crossed. And what really scared everyone at work, what made them pull me off the floor on Tuesday, is that I started just... freezing up in the middle of service. Like, totally stuck. Or I'd start doing things completely out of order. I was supposed to be closing out a party of six, but instead, I was just standing by the soda station. I was holding the key to the cash drawer, just gripping it, and I kept repeating table numbers out loud—\"table 42, table 12, table 42\"—instead of actually going to the register and closing their checks. And I knew I had the key, and I knew I needed to do the checks, but I just couldn't make my body do the next step, and I just kept saying the numbers over and over while the ice machine was humming, and the manager had to physically come take the key out of my hand because I was just... stuck there.\n\nDoctor: When you were repeating those numbers over and over, was there a specific thought or fear driving you to say them, or was it completely out of your control?\n\nPatient: It wasn't really a fear driving it, no. It was... well, like I was just saying, it was just completely out of my control. That's the part that really scared everyone, and me too, honestly. The freezing up. Because normally, closing checks is just pure muscle memory, right? You get the cash, you take the key, you go to the till, you make the change. It's a dance. But standing there by the soda station... it was like the connection between my brain and my feet just completely severed. \n\nI was holding that cash drawer key so tight my knuckles were white, and I could feel the little metal ridges on it, and the little plastic tag, but I couldn't make myself actually walk the fifteen feet over to the register. Voices... yeah. Yeah, actually. I haven't really told anyone at the restaurant about this part because I didn't want them to think... well, you know. But yes. Mostly it happens when we're closing up. You know, after the doors are locked and they finally turn off that awful overhead playlist we have to listen to all day? It gets really quiet in there, and I'll be in the back doing the drawer, counting out the cash and matching up the credit card slips. And while I'm doing that, I'll hear this woman. 

It's a woman's voice, and she'll just say my name. Or, a lot of the time, she'll tell me I'm doing the count wrong. She'll say something like, 'You're messing up the count,' just right in my ear. And it sounds so real, so completely normal and clear, that I'll actually talk back to her. I'll say out loud, 'Give me a second, I'm almost done,' or 'No I'm not, I just counted the twenties.' And then I'll turn around to see which of the girls is messing with me, and... there's just nobody there. The back room is completely empty. And it hits me that nobody else heard it, that I'm just talking to the air. 

And it happens when I'm at home, too, not just at work. Usually when I'm in my own kitchen. I'll just be standing there, maybe making some chamomile tea after a long shift or wiping down the counters—I always wipe down the counters before bed, it's a habit from the restaurant—and she'll call my name again. And I'll answer her! I'll say 'What?' or 'I'm in here!' out loud to my empty apartment. It's just so vivid that my brain doesn't even question it until after I've already answered, and then I'm just standing there in my kitchen realizing I'm completely alone.

Physical illnesses? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a bad cold or anything. I try to take really good care of myself, you know? Working in the service industry, you're around people all the time, constantly handling dirty plates and glasses, wiping down tables where people have been coughing and sneezing, especially in the winter. So I'm always washing my hands, always using the sanitizer by the POS system. I even take extra Vitamin C and zinc when I feel like the weather is changing, just to be safe. Because if you call out sick at the restaurant, it's this whole big ordeal where you have to find your own cover, and nobody ever wants to pick up a Tuesday night shift because the tips are usually pretty light, unless there's a big show across the street. 

So, no, I haven't been sick. I haven't had any infections. My stomach has been fine, no weird rashes or anything. I haven't even had a headache lately, which is surprising because the fluorescent lights in the back room usually give me a migraine by the end of the week. But physically? Physically, I feel completely fine. My body feels healthy. It's just... it's just the freezing up, and the voices, and the wires getting crossed. But my actual physical health, like catching a bug or something? No, definitely not. I haven't been to a regular doctor for a sick visit in... gosh, maybe two or three years? Not since I had that really bad sinus infection right before Thanksgiving, but that was ages ago. So, yeah, no physical illnesses at all.

Watched? Well... yeah. Actually, yes, I have. It’s funny you bring that up because I was just talking about this, or trying to talk about it, anyway. It’s mostly at work, again. I keep getting this really intense feeling that a couple of the other servers are just... staring at me. Watching my every move. Especially when I'm at the POS terminal. We have these three computer terminals, right? And I'll be standing there punching in an order, trying to make sure I get all the modifications right because the kitchen gets so incredibly mad if you don't use the exact right buttons for the modifiers, and I can just feel their eyes boring into the back of my neck. They are watching exactly how I ring things in. 

And it's not just the girls I work with, either. There's this one regular customer—he always comes in on Thursdays, usually sits at a two-top in section two, always gets the salmon—and he watches me too. He’ll just sit there, sipping his drink, and track me with his eyes the entire time I'm on the floor. 

I wouldn't even mind the staring so much, but I know what they're doing. I know why they're watching me. They're leaving these little signs on the tickets. The printed receipt tickets, you know? I’ll look at the chits that print out, and there will be these weird little marks on them, or the way the paper is torn at the top is completely deliberate, or there's an extra space between the items that shouldn't be there. It's like they're building this whole case against me. They're leaving these signs to prove I'm incompetent or messing up, and they're gathering it all up because they're trying to get me fired. 

I know how that sounds, but I actually started saving some of the duplicate tickets at the end of my shifts to prove it. I brought a whole handful of them home last week and laid them all out on the kitchen table to show my husband. I was pointing out all the little signs, showing him how they're all coordinating this whole thing to get management to let me go. But he just... he didn't get it at all. He just sighed and told me I was overthinking it. He said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, you know, like the printer glitching out, or someone just ripping the paper too fast because we're in the weeds and the expeditor is yelling. But Mark doesn't work there. He doesn't see the way they hover right behind me when there's another open terminal right by the bar they could easily use. He doesn't see how that regular customer watches my hands when I type. He just thinks it's normal restaurant drama, but I know they're leaving those signs on purpose.

It's just so vivid that my brain doesn't even question it until after I've already answered, and then I'm just standing there in my kitchen realizing I'm completely alone.\n\nDoctor: ```json\n{\"intent\": \"screen for underlying general medical conditions to differentiate organic catatonic disorder from primary psychiatric disorder\", \"question\": \"Have you recently had any physical illnesses, infections,\n\nPatient: Physical illnesses? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I haven't had a fever or a bad cold or anything. I try to take really good care of myself, you know? Working in the service industry, you're around people all the time, constantly handling dirty plates and glasses, wiping down tables where people have been coughing and sneezing, especially in the winter. So I'm always washing my hands, always using the sanitizer by the POS system. I even take extra Vitamin C and zinc when I feel like the weather is changing, just to be safe. Because if you call out sick at the restaurant, it's this whole big ordeal where you have to find your own cover, and nobody ever wants to pick up a Tuesday night shift because the tips are usually pretty light, unless there's a big show across the street. \n\nSo, no, I haven't been sick. I haven't had any infections. My stomach has been fine, no weird rashes or anything. I haven't even had a headache lately, which is surprising because the fluorescent lights in the back room usually give me a migraine by the end of the week. But physically? We have these three computer terminals, right? And I'll be standing there punching in an order, trying to make sure I get all the modifications right because the kitchen gets so incredibly mad if you don't use the exact right buttons for the modifiers, and I can just feel their eyes boring into the back of my neck. They are watching exactly how I ring things in. \n\nAnd it's not just the girls I work with, either. There's this one regular customer—he always comes in on Thursdays, usually sits at a two-top in section two, always gets the salmon—and he watches me too. He’ll just sit there, sipping his drink, and track me with his eyes the entire time I'm on the floor. \n\nI wouldn't even mind the staring so much, but I know what they're doing. I know why they're watching me. They're leaving these little signs on the tickets. The printed receipt tickets, you know? It's like they're building this whole case against me. They're leaving these signs to prove I'm incompetent or messing up, and they're gathering it all up because they're trying to get me fired. \n\nI know how that sounds, but I actually started saving some of the duplicate tickets at the end of my shifts to prove it. I brought a whole handful of them home last week and laid them all out on the kitchen table to show my husband. He just sighed and told me I was overthinking it. He said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, you know, like the printer glitching out, or someone just ripping the paper too fast because we're in the weeds and the expeditor is yelling. But Mark doesn't work there. He doesn't see the way they hover right behind me when there's another open terminal right by the bar they could easily use. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it wasn't just Tuesday. Tuesday was the worst one, the one that made Mark finally say I had to come see someone, but... no, it's been happening a lot lately. Actually, that's kind of what scared everyone at work. That's why I think they're trying to get me fired, because I keep... I keep freezing up right in the middle of service. 

It's like my brain just stops, but my body is still stuck doing whatever it was doing. Or I'll start doing things completely out of order, which you absolutely cannot do when you're in the weeds on a Friday night. Like, last weekend, I was supposed to be closing out checks, right? I had three tables that were ready to pay, and people get so impatient when they want to leave. But instead of going to the terminal and running the cards, I ended up just standing over by the soda station. I was just standing there, holding the little metal key for the cash drawer in my hand, and I couldn't move. I was just staring at the ice machine, and I started repeating table numbers out loud. Just saying 'Table 42, Table 12, Table 42, Table 12' over and over again. 

And I knew I was doing it, somewhere in the back of my head I knew I needed to go run those cards, but I just couldn't make my feet walk back to the POS system. One of the hostesses finally had to come over and physically touch my arm to snap me out of it, and she looked so freaked out. And that's not even the only time! A few weeks ago, I got stuck holding a tray of dirty glasses halfway between the floor and the dish pit for what felt like ten minutes. I just stopped walking and stood there like a statue. So, no, it definitely wasn't just Tuesday. It's happening more and more, and I just... I don't know how to stop it when it happens.

Sudden episodes of extreme, purposeless... like, moving around a lot? Or getting super agitated for no reason? 

No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm a server, so my whole job is basically moving around constantly, right? You're always on your feet, always walking from the kitchen to the floor to the dish pit, carrying trays, running drinks, dodging the busboys... so there's always a purpose to it. You have to be moving fast. But I don't just... start pacing or flailing or moving around for absolutely no reason. 

If anything, it's the exact opposite, like I was just saying with the freezing. When things get to be too much, or when I know they're watching me and plotting with those receipt tickets, I don't get hyper or physically agitated. I just... stop. All the energy just drains right out of me and I turn into a statue. Mark, my husband, he actually mentioned that the other day. He said when I get home from a bad shift, I don't pace around the house or anything, I just sit on the edge of the couch and stare at the coffee table for like an hour without moving a muscle. 

But I definitely don't have these sudden bursts of crazy movement. I can't even imagine what Dave—he's our general manager, this really strict guy who used to run a corporate chain and cares way too much about optics—I can't imagine what he would do if I just started moving around wildly in the middle of the dining room during the Friday dinner rush. He's already looking for a reason to let me go because of the freezing, so if I did something like that, he'd fire me on the spot. So, no, definitely nothing like that. Just the getting stuck.

Speak? Well... it depends. Like I said before, when I was standing over by the soda station, the time I was supposed to be running those credit cards for the three tables that were ready to leave... I was speaking then. But it wasn't... it wasn't like a conversation. I wasn't talking *to* anybody. I was just standing there, frozen, holding that little metal key for the cash drawer—which I didn't even need because I was supposed to be at the POS terminal, not the physical register—and I was just repeating the table numbers over and over. "Table 42, Table 12, Table 42, Table 12." 

So my mouth was working, the words were coming out, but it wasn't like I was choosing to say them to communicate. It was just... stuck on a loop, just like my body was stuck. And that's exactly what scared everyone at work so badly. It's not just the freezing up right in the middle of service, it's that I'm doing things completely out of order, or doing things that make absolutely no sense for what I'm supposed to be doing. Like standing by the ice machine repeating numbers instead of closing out checks. 

But other times... like when I was stuck holding that heavy tray of draft beer glasses halfway to the dish pit? I couldn't say a word. Mateo squeezed right past me and said "excuse me," and I wanted to say "sorry" or "watch out" or literally anything, but my throat just felt completely locked up. Like the signal from my brain just couldn't make it to my vocal cords. I was just a statue. So sometimes I'm babbling things that don't make sense, and sometimes I'm completely mute. It just depends on the episode, I guess. It's all just so messed up.

Instructions or guide me? Like telling me what to do? 

Well, I don't really know, because... well, I guess people don't really try to guide me or tell me what to do when I'm like that. I mean, like I was saying about Chloe tapping my elbow, she wasn't trying to give me instructions, she was just trying to snap me out of it. And Mateo just squeezed past me. "Table 42, Table 12." Instead of actually closing out the checks like I was supposed to be doing. 

So if someone *did* try to give me instructions right then, like if Dave the manager came over and said, "Hey, put the key down and go to the POS," I don't know if I would have listened. I don't know if I *could* have listened. I was just so stuck in that loop, doing the completely wrong thing, frozen in place. I think that's why they don't try to guide me. They just stare at me because it's so bizarre to see someone standing there doing something that makes absolutely zero sense for what they're supposed to be doing. So, no, nobody really gives me instructions. They just wait for me to snap out of it or they just walk around me.

And I'll be standing there punching in an order, trying to make sure I get all the modifications right because the kitchen gets so incredibly mad if you don't use the exact right buttons for the modifiers, and I can just feel their eyes boring into the back of my neck. They are watching exactly how I ring things in. \n\nAnd it's not just the girls I work with, either. There's this one regular customer—he always comes in on Thursdays, usually sits at a two-top in section two, always gets the salmon—and he watches me too. He’ll just sit there, sipping his drink, and track me with his eyes the entire time I'm on the floor. \n\nI wouldn't even mind the staring so much, but I know what they're doing. I know why they're watching me. They're leaving these little signs on the tickets. The printed receipt tickets, you know? I’ll look at the chits that print out, and there will be these weird little marks on them, or the way the paper is torn at the top is completely deliberate, or there's an extra space between the items that shouldn't be there. It's like they're building this whole case against me. They're leaving these signs to prove I'm incompetent or messing up, and they're gathering it all up because they're trying to get me fired. \n\nI know how that sounds, but I actually started saving some of the duplicate tickets at the end of my shifts to prove it. I brought a whole handful of them home last week and laid them all out on the kitchen table to show my husband. I was pointing out all the little signs, showing him how they're all coordinating this whole thing to get management to let me go. But he just... he didn't get it at all. He just sighed and told me I was overthinking it. He said it’s just normal restaurant mess-ups, you know, like the printer glitching out, or someone just ripping the paper too fast because we're in the weeds and the expeditor is yelling. He doesn't see how that regular customer watches my hands when I type.
